========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, yeah, thank you. I came in because I've been having a really hard time recently. I was working on this memorial piece for my aunt—she passed away a little while ago, and she was actually the one who convinced me to get my bachelor's in fine arts in the first place. We used to sit on her porch and paint watercolors when I was little. She had this massive wrap-around porch with all these ferns she could never keep alive, which is ironic because she painted them so beautifully... 

But anyway, I was working on her memorial piece, and I just kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like, it felt like if I made one wrong photo placement or picked the wrong color choice, it would literally mean I didn't love her enough. And I mean, I'm an artist, I get creative doubts all the time, but I couldn't just brush these off like normal. It was just... I don't know, I remember I ran out of my good archival glue right in the middle of it and had to drive all the way across town to the craft store in terrible traffic, and my mind was just racing the whole time about ruining her memory. So, yeah, that's why I decided to make an appointment.

I don't know, I remember I ran out of my good archival glue right in the middle of it and had to drive all the way across town to the craft store in terrible traffic, and my mind was just racing the whole time about ruining her memory. So, yeah, that's why I decided to make an appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these kinds of intrusive, unwanted thoughts in situations other than working on your aunt’s memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, not that I can think of. It really just started with the memorial piece. I haven't really noticed it with anything else. Although, I did have a hard time deciding what to make for dinner last night, but that's just because I'm terrible at cooking. My partner usually cooks, actually. He makes this amazing vegetarian lasagna. He uses, like, three different kinds of cheese, and he roasts the vegetables first so they get all caramelized. It takes him hours, but it's so worth it. We actually had it for our anniversary last month. We went to this little Italian place for our first date, so it's kind of a tradition now. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't really had those thoughts outside of the memorial project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty making decisions or feeling anxious about making mistakes in other areas of your life, even outside of your art?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more anxious about mistakes than anyone else usually. Like, I said, I'm bad at cooking, so I guess I get a little stressed if I have to follow a new recipe. But that's just normal, right? Actually, speaking of recipes, I was trying to bake bread the other day. Sourdough, you know? Everyone got into it a few years ago, but I just started. It's so finicky! You have to feed the starter, and it smells kind of like beer, which reminds me of my dad. He used to brew his own beer in the garage when I was a teenager. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.

Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.

I mean, not that I can think of. It really just started with the memorial piece. I haven't really noticed it with anything else. Although, I did have a hard time deciding what to make for dinner last night, but that's just because I'm terrible at cooking. My partner usually cooks, actually. He makes this amazing vegetarian lasagna. He uses, like, three different kinds of cheese, and he roasts the vegetables first so they get all caramelized. It takes him hours, but it's so worth it. We actually had it for our anniversary last month. We went to this little Italian place for our first date, so it's kind of a tradition now. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't really had those thoughts outside of the memorial project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty making decisions or feeling anxious about making mistakes in other areas of your life, even outside of your art?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more anxious about mistakes than anyone else usually. Like, I said, I'm bad at cooking, so I guess I get a little stressed if I have to follow a new recipe. But that's just normal, right? Actually, speaking of recipes, I was trying to bake bread the other day. Sourdough, you know? Everyone got into it a few years ago, but I just started. It's so finicky! You have to feed the starter, and it smells kind of like beer, which reminds me of my dad. He used to brew his own beer in the garage when I was a teenager. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about the memorial piece cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life, like making it hard to focus on anything else or affecting your sleep or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. Um, no, not really. I mean, deciding what to wear or what to watch on TV is pretty easy for me. So, yeah, that's why I decided to make an appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these kinds of intrusive, unwanted thoughts in situations other than working on your aunt’s memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, not that I can think of. It really just started with the memorial piece. I haven't really noticed it with anything else. Although, I did have a hard time deciding what to make for dinner last night, but that's just because I'm terrible at cooking. My partner usually cooks, actually. He makes this amazing vegetarian lasagna. He uses, like, three different kinds of cheese, and he roasts the vegetables first so they get all caramelized. It takes him hours, but it's so worth it. We actually had it for our anniversary last month. We went to this little Italian place for our first date, so it's kind of a tradition now. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't really had those thoughts outside of the memorial project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty making decisions or feeling anxious about making mistakes in other areas of your life, even outside of your art?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more anxious about mistakes than anyone else usually. Like, I said, I'm bad at cooking, so I guess I get a little stressed if I have to follow a new recipe. But that's just normal, right? Actually, speaking of recipes, I was trying to bake bread the other day. Sourdough, you know? Everyone got into it a few years ago, but I just started. It's so finicky! You have to feed the starter, and it smells kind of like beer, which reminds me of my dad. He used to brew his own beer in the garage when I was a teenager. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about the memorial piece cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life, like making it hard to focus on anything else or affecting your sleep or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't really have that kind of intense anxiety about anything else. Just the sculpture.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your art and cooking, have you noticed any significant anxiety or difficulty when making everyday decisions, even small ones?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. They're so soft, and they have all these extra pockets which are great for holding my brushes. I found them in this tiny little shop on the east side of town. The owner there is this older guy who plays jazz music all day on a record player. It's such a cool vibe. He actually had a whole stack of old Miles Davis records by the register. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. Um, no, not really. If I make a typo in an email, I usually just send a quick 'oops' and move on. It happens to everyone, right? Actually, speaking of emails, I've been getting an insane amount of spam lately. It's just ridiculous. The other day I got one claiming I won a free cruise to the Bahamas. I obviously knew it was a scam, but it really got me thinking about the beach. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. 

I went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. My mom used to walk down the beach every morning to collect seashells and put them in glass jars on the windowsill. She had this one specific jar that was just full of sea glass—all these beautiful, frosted greens and pale blues. I actually tried to incorporate some sea glass into a mixed-media piece I did back in college, but the epoxy I used totally yellowed over time and ruined the whole aesthetic. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. 

But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.

We used to sit on her porch and paint watercolors when I was little. She had this massive wrap-around porch with all these ferns she could never keep alive, which is ironic because she painted them so beautifully... \n\nBut anyway, I was working on her memorial piece, and I just kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like, it felt like if I made one wrong photo placement or picked the wrong color choice, it would literally mean I didn't love her enough. And I mean, I'm an artist, I get creative doubts all the time, but I couldn't just brush these off like normal. It was just... I don't know, I remember I ran out of my good archival glue right in the middle of it and had to drive all the way across town to the craft store in terrible traffic, and my mind was just racing the whole time about ruining her memory. So, yeah, that's why I decided to make an appointment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these kinds of intrusive, unwanted thoughts in situations other than working on your aunt’s memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, not that I can think of. It really just started with the memorial piece. I haven't really noticed it with anything else. Although, I did have a hard time deciding what to make for dinner last night, but that's just because I'm terrible at cooking. My partner usually cooks, actually. He makes this amazing vegetarian lasagna. He uses, like, three different kinds of cheese, and he roasts the vegetables first so they get all caramelized. It takes him hours, but it's so worth it. We actually had it for our anniversary last month. We went to this little Italian place for our first date, so it's kind of a tradition now. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't really had those thoughts outside of the memorial project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty making decisions or feeling anxious about making mistakes in other areas of your life, even outside of your art?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm any more anxious about mistakes than anyone else usually. Like, I said, I'm bad at cooking, so I guess I get a little stressed if I have to follow a new recipe. But that's just normal, right? Actually, speaking of recipes, I was trying to bake bread the other day. Sourdough, you know? Everyone got into it a few years ago, but I just started. It's so finicky! You have to feed the starter, and it smells kind of like beer, which reminds me of my dad. He used to brew his own beer in the garage when I was a teenager. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about the memorial piece cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life, like making it hard to focus on anything else or affecting your sleep or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't really have that kind of intense anxiety about anything else. Just the sculpture.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your art and cooking, have you noticed any significant anxiety or difficulty when making everyday decisions, even small ones?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, deciding what to wear or what to watch on TV is pretty easy for me. I usually just throw on whatever's comfortable anyway, like these overalls I got from a thrift store last week. They're so soft, and they have all these extra pockets which are great for holding my brushes. I found them in this tiny little shop on the east side of town. The owner there is this older guy who plays jazz music all day on a record player. It's such a cool vibe. He actually had a whole stack of old Miles Davis records by the register. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. I keep meaning to look it up online, but then I get distracted by something else. But yeah, no big anxiety about small decisions. Just normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or upset about making small, everyday mistakes, even things like sending an email with a typo or wearing something mismatched?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. If I make a typo in an email, I usually just send a quick 'oops' and move on. It happens to everyone, right? Actually, speaking of emails, I've been getting an insane amount of spam lately. It's just ridiculous. The other day I got one claiming I won a free cruise to the Bahamas. I obviously knew it was a scam, but it really got me thinking about the beach. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. \n\nI went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. My mom used to walk down the beach every morning to collect seashells and put them in glass jars on the windowsill. She had this one specific jar that was just full of sea glass—all these beautiful, frosted greens and pale blues. I actually tried to incorporate some sea glass into a mixed-media piece I did back in college, but the epoxy I used totally yellowed over time and ruined the whole aesthetic. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt anxious or upset about things not being done perfectly, even in small or seemingly unimportant ways, like a door not being fully closed or a line in a drawing not being straight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I mean, if a door isn't shut all the way, I might just close it if there's a draft, but it doesn't upset me. And a crooked line in a drawing? Honestly, sometimes that's the best part. I love happy accidents in art. It reminds me of this time I was taking a pottery class—just for fun, you know, to get my hands dirty. I was trying to throw this perfectly symmetrical vase on the wheel, but my elbow slipped and I totally gouged the side of the clay. I thought it was ruined, but the instructor came over and showed me how to carve into the mistake and make it look intentional. It ended up being my favorite piece from the whole class. I painted it this incredible cobalt blue, but the glaze fired a little weird in the kiln, so it came out looking almost metallic. I actually gave it to my sister for her birthday last year. She has it sitting on her mantelpiece right next to this awful, creepy antique clock her husband bought at a flea market. It chimes every hour and it sounds like something out of a horror movie. I told her she should just take the batteries out, but she says it gives the room character. I don't know how she sleeps through it, honestly. But yeah, no, things don't have to be perfect for me. I'm pretty relaxed about that kind of stuff.

I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. \n\nI went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. My mom used to walk down the beach every morning to collect seashells and put them in glass jars on the windowsill. She had this one specific jar that was just full of sea glass—all these beautiful, frosted greens and pale blues. I actually tried to incorporate some sea glass into a mixed-media piece I did back in college, but the epoxy I used totally yellowed over time and ruined the whole aesthetic. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or upset about things not being done perfectly, even in small or seemingly unimportant ways, like a door not being fully closed or a line in a drawing not being straight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, if a door isn't shut all the way, I might just close it if there's a draft, but it doesn't upset me. And a crooked line in a drawing? Honestly, sometimes that's the best part. I love happy accidents in art. It reminds me of this time I was taking a pottery class—just for fun, you know, to get my hands dirty. I was trying to throw this perfectly symmetrical vase on the wheel, but my elbow slipped and I totally gouged the side of the clay. I thought it was ruined, but the instructor came over and showed me how to carve into the mistake and make it look intentional. It ended up being my favorite piece from the whole class. I painted it this incredible cobalt blue, but the glaze fired a little weird in the kiln, so it came out looking almost metallic. I actually gave it to my sister for her birthday last year. She has it sitting on her mantelpiece right next to this awful, creepy antique clock her husband bought at a flea market. It chimes every hour and it sounds like something out of a horror movie. I told her she should just take the batteries out, but she says it gives the room character. I don't know how she sleeps through it, honestly. But yeah, no, things don't have to be perfect for me. I'm pretty relaxed about that kind of stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any anxiety, distress, or intrusive thoughts that come up in response to things other than the memorial piece—even if they don't seem related to art or decisions?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. She had this massive wrap-around porch with all these ferns she could never keep alive, which is ironic because she painted them so beautifully... \n\nBut anyway, I was working on her memorial piece, and I just kept getting these awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like, it felt like if I made one wrong photo placement or picked the wrong color choice, it would literally mean I didn't love her enough. I don't know, I remember I ran out of my good archival glue right in the middle of it and had to drive all the way across town to the craft store in terrible traffic, and my mind was just racing the whole time about ruining her memory. He makes this amazing vegetarian lasagna. He uses, like, three different kinds of cheese, and he roasts the vegetables first so they get all caramelized. It takes him hours, but it's so worth it. You have to feed the starter, and it smells kind of like beer, which reminds me of my dad. He used to brew his own beer in the garage when I was a teenager. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about the memorial piece cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life, like making it hard to focus on anything else or affecting your sleep or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't really have that kind of intense anxiety about anything else. Just the sculpture.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your art and cooking, have you noticed any significant anxiety or difficulty when making everyday decisions, even small ones?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, deciding what to wear or what to watch on TV is pretty easy for me. I usually just throw on whatever's comfortable anyway, like these overalls I got from a thrift store last week. They're so soft, and they have all these extra pockets which are great for holding my brushes. I found them in this tiny little shop on the east side of town. The owner there is this older guy who plays jazz music all day on a record player. It's such a cool vibe. He actually had a whole stack of old Miles Davis records by the register. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. I keep meaning to look it up online, but then I get distracted by something else. But yeah, no big anxiety about small decisions. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. \n\nI went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. I actually tried to incorporate some sea glass into a mixed-media piece I did back in college, but the epoxy I used totally yellowed over time and ruined the whole aesthetic. I have this one picture of her on my fridge, actually, and she's standing in her backyard by these massive hydrangea bushes she grew. She was so proud of them. \n\nI actually tried to grow hydrangeas on my apartment balcony last summer because of that picture, but it was a complete disaster. I think they need way more space, or maybe different soil? I ended up buying this really expensive organic potting mix from the nursery downtown, and I lugged the whole giant bag up three flights of stairs by myself. My arms were sore for like two days. I ended up just using it to repot my snake plant, which is literally the only thing I can keep alive. Honestly, I think my apartment just doesn't get enough natural light because of this huge oak tree right outside my living room window. The squirrels in that tree are so loud in the morning, they literally wake me up before my alarm goes off. They throw acorns at the glass, I swear they do it on purpose... \n\nBut wait, sorry. You were asking about my aunt? Yeah, no, just regular missing her when she comes to mind. No weird anxiety or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you have those intrusive thoughts about dishonoring your aunt while working on the memorial piece, do you feel driven to do something over and over again—like checking, redoing parts, or following certain rules—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels ‘settled,’ even though part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah, I guess I do do that. When I get that feeling, like I'm messing up her memorial, I just... I can't leave it alone. I'll end up rereading the label text I wrote for the piece, like, over and over again. And then I'll start nudging the objects on the display table, just moving them a fraction of an inch this way or that way, and checking the layout photos I took on my phone repeatedly until it finally feels, I don't know, "settled." Even though part of me knows it's already fine, and I'm just completely wasting my studio time. 

Which is so frustrating because studio time is really expensive right now. My landlord just raised the rent on my space by like fifteen percent, which feels totally illegal, but what can you do? I share the space with this other artist, Mark, who does these massive, abstract metal sculptures. He's always using the welding torch, and it smells terrible, like burning ozone or something. Plus, he never takes the trash out. I'm always the one hauling the bins down to the alley. Last week, I found a half-eaten sandwich sitting on his workbench for three days. Three days! I finally had to throw it away because it was attracting fruit flies. I tried making those little traps with apple cider vinegar and dish soap—have you ever tried that? It works surprisingly well, but it smells like a salad dressing exploded in the room. 

Anyway, sorry. But yeah, I do get stuck redoing and checking the memorial stuff until it feels right, even when I know I shouldn't.

I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. The owner there is this older guy who plays jazz music all day on a record player. It's such a cool vibe. He actually had a whole stack of old Miles Davis records by the register. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. \n\nI went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or upset about things not being done perfectly, even in small or seemingly unimportant ways, like a door not being fully closed or a line in a drawing not being straight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I thought it was ruined, but the instructor came over and showed me how to carve into the mistake and make it look intentional. It ended up being my favorite piece from the whole class. I painted it this incredible cobalt blue, but the glaze fired a little weird in the kiln, so it came out looking almost metallic. I actually gave it to my sister for her birthday last year. She has it sitting on her mantelpiece right next to this awful, creepy antique clock her husband bought at a flea market. It chimes every hour and it sounds like something out of a horror movie. I mean, if a door isn't shut all the way, I might just close it if there's a draft, but it doesn't upset me. And a crooked line in a drawing? Honestly, sometimes that's the best part. I love happy accidents in art. It reminds me of this time I was taking a pottery class—just for fun, you know, to get my hands dirty. I was trying to throw this perfectly symmetrical vase on the wheel, but my elbow slipped and I totally gouged the side of the clay. I thought it was ruined, but the instructor came over and showed me how to carve into the mistake and make it look intentional. It ended up being my favorite piece from the whole class. I painted it this incredible cobalt blue, but the glaze fired a little weird in the kiln, so it came out looking almost metallic. I actually gave it to my sister for her birthday last year. She has it sitting on her mantelpiece right next to this awful, creepy antique clock her husband bought at a flea market. It chimes every hour and it sounds like something out of a horror movie. I told her she should just take the batteries out, but she says it gives the room character. I don't know how she sleeps through it, honestly. But yeah, no, things don't have to be perfect for me. I'm pretty relaxed about that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety, distress, or intrusive thoughts that come up in response to things other than the memorial piece—even if they don't seem related to art or decisions?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, besides the memorial piece, I don't really have any intrusive thoughts or, like, weird distress about other stuff. My mind is usually pretty calm, unless I'm trying to figure out what to make for dinner. Speaking of dinner, I went to this new Thai place downtown last night with my friend Sarah. Oh my gosh, the pad thai was incredible, but the portions were so huge! I brought half of it home and put it in the fridge, but my roommate's cat somehow managed to open the container. I swear, that cat is a genius or something. He's this massive orange tabby named Barnaby, and he knows how to open cabinets and doors. The other day, I caught him trying to turn the knob on the bathroom door while I was in the shower. It's hilarious, but also kind of terrifying. Sarah thinks I should get a lock for the fridge, but I feel like that's a bit extreme, right? Anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, anxiety about other things. Yeah, no, I don't think so. Just normal everyday stuff, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety, distress, or intrusive thoughts when you're reminded of your aunt, even outside of working on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I think about her, it's mostly just... normal sad, you know? Like I just miss her. I have this one picture of her on my fridge, actually, and she's standing in her backyard by these massive hydrangea bushes she grew. She was so proud of them. \n\nI actually tried to grow hydrangeas on my apartment balcony last summer because of that picture, but it was a complete disaster. I think they need way more space, or maybe different soil? I ended up buying this really expensive organic potting mix from the nursery downtown, and I lugged the whole giant bag up three flights of stairs by myself. My arms were sore for like two days. I ended up just using it to repot my snake plant, which is literally the only thing I can keep alive. Honestly, I think my apartment just doesn't get enough natural light because of this huge oak tree right outside my living room window. You were asking about my aunt? Yeah, no, just regular missing her when she comes to mind. No weird anxiety or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: When you have those intrusive thoughts about dishonoring your aunt while working on the memorial piece, do you feel driven to do something over and over again—like checking, redoing parts, or following certain rules—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I do do that. When I get that feeling, like I'm messing up her memorial, I just... I can't leave it alone. I'll end up rereading the label text I wrote for the piece, like, over and over again. It made the whole house smell like yeast and hops. He had all these glass carboys lined up... he even made his own labels for the bottles. He was a graphic designer, so they looked really professional. But anyway, yeah, I don't think I have any major issues with decisions or mistakes outside of that memorial piece. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on art projects other than the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense anxiety or worry that a small mistake might ruin the whole thing?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, like I said earlier, I get normal creative doubts, but nothing intense. Usually, if I mess up a painting, I just paint over it or turn it into something else. That's the beauty of acrylics, they dry so fast. I actually prefer watercolors, though, like the ones I used to do with my aunt. But watercolors are so unforgiving. If you make a mistake with those, you're kind of stuck with it. I tried to teach my niece how to use them last weekend when she came over. She's six, and she just wanted to mix all the colors together until they turned brown. She ended up painting her hands more than the paper. We had to use so much soap to get it off, and it was that nice lavender soap I bought at the farmer's market. There's this woman there who makes all her own soaps and lotions, and she brings her golden retriever to her booth. He's so sweet, he just sleeps under the table the whole time. But yeah, with my regular art, I don't really get that intense worry. Just with the memorial piece.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about the memorial piece cause you a lot of distress or interfere with your daily life, like making it hard to focus on anything else or affecting your sleep or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely causes some distress, yeah. I mean, I’m still getting most of my regular work done, but... I just lose so much extra time in the studio now. Like, I’ll be in there for hours just staring at it, trying to get it right. It actually already cost me a grant deadline I was supposed to apply for. The application was so long, anyway. They wanted, like, a ten-page artist statement and a detailed budget. I hate doing budgets. Numbers just kind of blur together for me. My accountant always yells at me because I keep all my receipts in this giant shoebox. It’s an old Nike box from when I tried to take up running a few years ago. I bought all the gear, the shoes, the moisture-wicking shirts... and I think I ran for maybe two weeks before I gave up. It’s just so boring, you know? Just putting one foot in front of the other. I'd rather be doing something creative. But anyway, yeah, missing that grant really sucked. And I also had to back out of a gallery talk I had actually really wanted to do, just because I felt so behind and stressed about the piece. So, I guess it is affecting things a bit.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, have you experienced any other situations where you felt intense distress or anxiety that interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. Like I said, it's pretty much just the memorial piece that's got me all tied up in knots. I mean, I get stressed sometimes, like anyone else, but nothing that messes up my whole day. Actually, speaking of my day, I've been trying to get better at organizing my schedule. I bought this really nice planner from a stationery shop downtown. It's got this beautiful leather cover, kind of a deep burgundy color. The shop owner told me it was handmade in Italy. She's so nice, she always remembers my name when I go in. She has this huge collection of vintage fountain pens, too. I bought one from her once, thinking I'd get into calligraphy, but it's so messy. The ink gets everywhere, especially under my fingernails, and it takes days to wash out. I guess I'm just more of a paintbrush kind of person. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't really have that kind of intense anxiety about anything else. Just the sculpture.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your art and cooking, have you noticed any significant anxiety or difficulty when making everyday decisions, even small ones?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. They're so soft, and they have all these extra pockets which are great for holding my brushes. I found them in this tiny little shop on the east side of town. The owner there is this older guy who plays jazz music all day on a record player. It's such a cool vibe. He actually had a whole stack of old Miles Davis records by the register. I almost bought one, but then I remembered my record player has been broken for months. The needle snapped when I was trying to dust it, and I just haven't gotten around to ordering a new one. I keep meaning to look it up online, but then I get distracted by something else. But yeah, no big anxiety about small decisions. Just normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or upset about making small, everyday mistakes, even things like sending an email with a typo or wearing something mismatched?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. If I make a typo in an email, I usually just send a quick 'oops' and move on. It happens to everyone, right? Actually, speaking of emails, I've been getting an insane amount of spam lately. It's just ridiculous. The other day I got one claiming I won a free cruise to the Bahamas. I obviously knew it was a scam, but it really got me thinking about the beach. I haven't been to the ocean in... wow, years. \n\nI went to Cape Cod when I was a kid with my parents, and we stayed in this adorable little rented cottage that had these bright blue shutters. My mom used to walk down the beach every morning to collect seashells and put them in glass jars on the windowsill. She had this one specific jar that was just full of sea glass—all these beautiful, frosted greens and pale blues. I actually tried to incorporate some sea glass into a mixed-media piece I did back in college, but the epoxy I used totally yellowed over time and ruined the whole aesthetic. I was so mad, I had to scrap the entire project. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, typos and everyday mistakes. Yeah, no, little things like that don't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or upset about things not being done perfectly, even in small or seemingly unimportant ways, like a door not being fully closed or a line in a drawing not being straight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, if a door isn't shut all the way, I might just close it if there's a draft, but it doesn't upset me. And a crooked line in a drawing? Honestly, sometimes that's the best part. I love happy accidents in art. It reminds me of this time I was taking a pottery class—just for fun, you know, to get my hands dirty. I was trying to throw this perfectly symmetrical vase on the wheel, but my elbow slipped and I totally gouged the side of the clay. I thought it was ruined, but the instructor came over and showed me how to carve into the mistake and make it look intentional. It ended up being my favorite piece from the whole class. I painted it this incredible cobalt blue, but the glaze fired a little weird in the kiln, so it came out looking almost metallic. I actually gave it to my sister for her birthday last year. She has it sitting on her mantelpiece right next to this awful, creepy antique clock her husband bought at a flea market. It chimes every hour and it sounds like something out of a horror movie. I told her she should just take the batteries out, but she says it gives the room character. I don't know how she sleeps through it, honestly. But yeah, no, things don't have to be perfect for me. I'm pretty relaxed about that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety, distress, or intrusive thoughts that come up in response to things other than the memorial piece—even if they don't seem related to art or decisions?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, besides the memorial piece, I don't really have any intrusive thoughts or, like, weird distress about other stuff. My mind is usually pretty calm, unless I'm trying to figure out what to make for dinner. Speaking of dinner, I went to this new Thai place downtown last night with my friend Sarah. Oh my gosh, the pad thai was incredible, but the portions were so huge! I brought half of it home and put it in the fridge, but my roommate's cat somehow managed to open the container. I swear, that cat is a genius or something. He's this massive orange tabby named Barnaby, and he knows how to open cabinets and doors. The other day, I caught him trying to turn the knob on the bathroom door while I was in the shower. It's hilarious, but also kind of terrifying. Sarah thinks I should get a lock for the fridge, but I feel like that's a bit extreme, right? Anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, anxiety about other things. Yeah, no, I don't think so. Just normal everyday stuff, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety, distress, or intrusive thoughts when you're reminded of your aunt, even outside of working on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I think about her, it's mostly just... normal sad, you know? Like I just miss her. I have this one picture of her on my fridge, actually, and she's standing in her backyard by these massive hydrangea bushes she grew. She was so proud of them. \n\nI actually tried to grow hydrangeas on my apartment balcony last summer because of that picture, but it was a complete disaster. I think they need way more space, or maybe different soil? I ended up buying this really expensive organic potting mix from the nursery downtown, and I lugged the whole giant bag up three flights of stairs by myself. My arms were sore for like two days. I ended up just using it to repot my snake plant, which is literally the only thing I can keep alive. Honestly, I think my apartment just doesn't get enough natural light because of this huge oak tree right outside my living room window. The squirrels in that tree are so loud in the morning, they literally wake me up before my alarm goes off. They throw acorns at the glass, I swear they do it on purpose... \n\nBut wait, sorry. You were asking about my aunt? Yeah, no, just regular missing her when she comes to mind. No weird anxiety or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: When you have those intrusive thoughts about dishonoring your aunt while working on the memorial piece, do you feel driven to do something over and over again—like checking, redoing parts, or following certain rules—to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess I do do that. When I get that feeling, like I'm messing up her memorial, I just... I can't leave it alone. I'll end up rereading the label text I wrote for the piece, like, over and over again. And then I'll start nudging the objects on the display table, just moving them a fraction of an inch this way or that way, and checking the layout photos I took on my phone repeatedly until it finally feels, I don't know, \"settled.\" Even though part of me knows it's already fine, and I'm just completely wasting my studio time. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because studio time is really expensive right now. My landlord just raised the rent on my space by like fifteen percent, which feels totally illegal, but what can you do? I share the space with this other artist, Mark, who does these massive, abstract metal sculptures. He's always using the welding torch, and it smells terrible, like burning ozone or something. Plus, he never takes the trash out. I'm always the one hauling the bins down to the alley. Last week, I found a half-eaten sandwich sitting on his workbench for three days. Three days! I finally had to throw it away because it was attracting fruit flies. I tried making those little traps with apple cider vinegar and dish soap—have you ever tried that? It works surprisingly well, but it smells like a salad dressing exploded in the room. \n\nAnyway, sorry. But yeah, I do get stuck redoing and checking the memorial stuff until it feels right, even when I know I shouldn't.\n\nDoctor: Outside of working on the memorial piece, do you ever feel intense distress that something bad might happen if things aren’t done a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Outside of the memorial piece, I don't get that feeling that something bad is gonna happen if I don't do things a specific way. I'm actually pretty laid back about most things. Like, my apartment is kind of a mess right now, to be honest. I was supposed to organize my closet this weekend, but I ended up binge-watching this British baking show instead. It's so relaxing! Have you seen it? The judges are so polite, even when someone's cake completely collapses. \n\nIt made me want to try baking, so I went to the store and bought all this flour and sugar, but then I realized my oven is broken. The landlord was supposed to fix it three weeks ago. He keeps saying he's waiting on a part, but I see him outside watering his lawn every morning, so I don't know how hard he's really trying. His lawn does look amazing, though. It's like a golf course. He uses this special fertilizer that smells awful for the first day, but then it's fine. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, no. I don't really get that intense distress about other things having to be a certain way. Just the memorial piece.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
